The single place to start. It gathers what has been learned and decided, says how sure we are of each thing, and points to the document that holds the detail. Updated 2026-11-12. Update it whenever a decision is made or a fact is checked.

2. Words we use
Word Meaning
Factory One on-chain output shared by a host and many members, from which each member gets a Lightning channel
Host The party that funds a factory, co-signs everything in it and links it to the rest of Lightning. The SuperScalar papers call this the LSP
Member Someone with a channel in a factory. The papers call this the client
Tree The pre-signed transactions that divide the shared output down to each member's channel
Leaf The last transaction on a member's branch, holding their channel and some of the host's spare liquidity
Layer A level of the tree whose state can be replaced, using shrinking timelocks (Decker-Wattenhofer). Each adds about three days to an exit at mainnet settings
Pseudo-Spilman leaf A leaf whose states are chained, each spending the last, so it needs no timelock
Host's spare liquidity (L-stock) Funds on a leaf that the host can sell to a member as receiving capacity
Exit Leaving a factory. Cooperative: everyone signs one transaction. Alone (unilateral): the member publishes their branch
Rotation Moving members from a factory near its end into a new one
Ladder Running many factories with staggered end dates, a new one starting each day
Signing round One group signature: propose, exchange nonces, exchange partial signatures, combine
Lobby The waiting room where members gather before a factory is created
Terms The rules a host publishes for a factory
Safety rule The member-side check before every signature: "after this, can I still leave alone with at least what I am owed?"
Core Gazpacho's library that decides everything and touches nothing outside itself
Port A replaceable connection from the core to the outside: storage, chain view, network
Engine One of the core's decision-makers: signing round, channel, payment, exit, watch, lifecycle, schedule
Held payment An incoming Lightning payment a node accepts but does not settle until told to
SIP SuperScalar Improvement Proposal: the written request, discussion and decision record for a rule or feature. Categories: Rule, Protocol, Process, Informational
Target, minimum, deadline A lobby's start rule: start at once at the target; at the deadline start if the minimum is met, otherwise cancel
Never say "Staking". Say hosting, providing liquidity, fees
3. How SuperScalar is meant to work

From ZmnSCPxj's posts on Delving Bitcoin (topics 1143 and 1242) as read in earlier research. Not re-read today.

  • One host and many members share one on-chain output that needs everyone's signature.
  • The host funds everything. Members start at zero and are paid in over Lightning.
  • A tree of pre-signed transactions gives each member an exit that needs nobody's help.
  • Upper layers can be re-signed to move the host's spare liquidity between members. Leaves near the bottom need only a few members online to change.
  • When a factory expires, members are meant to receive everything and the host nothing ("inversion of the timeout default"), which makes keeping the service running the host's financial duty.
  • Factories last about 30 days plus about 3 days of winding down. A new one starts daily. Members move to a new factory by paying across over Lightning.
  • A payment's final deadline must be at least the tree's total exit delay plus a margin.
  • Arity 2 is suggested near the leaves, wider higher up, and fixed (stateless) near the root.
  • Pseudo-Spilman leaves were designed for several members per leaf sharing the host's liquidity. The reference code builds that shape too (corrected 2026-11-11): factory_set_ps_subfactory_arity(K) gives K members per sub-factory sharing the spare liquidity and K sub-factories per leaf (K² members), and factory_subfactory_chain_advance_unsigned sells liquidity by extending the chain, with a penalty for a stale chain. Both are in Gazpacho's import (core/superscalar/src/factory.c:1059, :2971). The default is K = 1, and the SuperScalar programs switch sales and advances off for now (#478, #481). Known divergence (corrected again 2026-11-11 by the SuperScalar shared-leaf study, and confirmed in our copy at factory.c around line 3007): every sale, the first included, spends all K+1 outputs of the previous step and grows the buyer's existing channel, so every channel on that sub-factory moves. The designer's shape, planned by SuperScalar for 1.1.0, is one input (the spare liquidity) and two outputs: a new channel for the buyer and the remaining stock. Channels then never move, and a member can hold several channels. Also found by the study, all to fix before shared leaves: the stale-chain penalty probably fires on honest exits; the exit-time check ignores K; the member-to-leaf mapping is wrong for K = 3 at 64 to 512 members; no second-spend guard on the shared stock; a host "funding reorged" message resets sale state without the member checking the chain. The orchestration around it (lsp_subfactory_chain_advance) is program code Gazpacho does not import.
4. What the reference code does today

Tested or read; see RECORD.md sections 16 and 18 and the hand-off note.

Fact How we know
Creation, payments and cooperative close work for 4 members on regtest Run on 2026-11-11
It has run 127 members on signet The project's own published record; not re-run by us
A member cannot complete an exit alone on main: the channel transaction's fee is below the relay minimum. The SuperScalar session reports that its draft pull request 476 fixes this (the exit relays as a package with a fee-paying child) Run on 2026-11-11; fix reported by the other session, not re-run by us
Factories above 16 members in the reference code use several users per leaf on an older leaf type. One-member pseudo-Spilman leaves under wide upper levels have not been shown to work at 64, 127 or 512 members Reported by the SuperScalar session on 2026-11-11 from its own runs
The size figures in the shared headers (256 signers, 256 leaves, 512 tree transactions) are only defaults at the current import: the tree grows past them. One member per pseudo-Spilman leaf under wide upper levels builds, signs and verifies at 64, 127, 255, 512 and 1000 members, in one process with all keys Built and run by us on 2026-11-11; under independent review
Exit delay is 432 blocks (3 days) per timed level on a member's path; the leaf adds none. At 512 members: 3,888 blocks with two-way levels, 1,296 with two 16-way levels, 432 with those two levels fixed Same run
Limits that remain in the shared code: a level is at most 16 wide (15 if the node keeps its fee anchor); at most 8 levels; at most 8 timed layers; a pre-made nonce pool of 256 Same run
The shared code silently turns a small subtree under a wide level into one multi-member leaf, so small factories need different shapes from large ones Same run
The host funds before the tree is signed Read in the code by the earlier session
Only the funding output pays members at expiry; every tree node still has a host-only timeout branch Read in the code by the earlier session
Payment deadlines are fixed at 80, 40 and 18 blocks, far shorter than an exit Read in the code by the earlier session
About 100 message types, in a private format Read on 2026-11-11
16 low-level files build and run alone and reproduce real transaction IDs Built and tested on 2026-11-11
Member order in the tree can depend on the C library's sort Read on 2026-11-11
Individual partial signatures are never verified Read on 2026-11-11
Randomness is read directly from the operating system, including for signing nonces Read on 2026-11-11
The earlier session found and fixed several ways for a host to take members' funds (close paying only the host, liquidity change emptying a balance, and others) That session's work; fixes are being merged there
5. Decisions in force

Newest first within each area. The date is when the owner decided.

Product

  • Gazpacho is both host and member software, self-contained, C, signet first, quality before dates. (11 Nov)
  • Soup wallet is mothballed, kept for reference. The SuperScalar repo is maintained by another session. (11 Nov)
  • Home is github.com/gazpacho-wallet/gazpacho, MIT, public since 11 Nov 2026.
  • The screens are a local web interface served by the C program. No wallet-in-a-web-page. (11 Nov)
  • No role question; everyone can host; hosting always visible. Fees in 1.0.0 (proposed, owner to confirm): per payment, plus an optional joining fee from the first payment received. Payout to one wallet in 1.0.0, derived per-factory addresses in 1.1.0, outside wallet later. Receiving room, splicing, on-demand channels and shared leaves with sales are 1.1.0. Detail in ux/decisions.md, summary in DESIGN.md and PLAN.md. (11 Nov)
  • Interface: layout concept H is the base, with the critique's fixes and the owner's answers still to apply. (12 Nov)

Construction

  • 1.0.0 trees are advanceless (owner, 2026-11-12, questions 4): every interior level fixed, no timed (Decker-Wattenhofer) levels; timed levels and layer updates arrive with 1.1.0 and liquidity sales. This replaces the earlier "full layered tree in the first release". 1.0.0 seats are free (protected by invitation-only lobbies, host removal and standbys), then a proof of a coin or channel, then a per-lobby choice of options. Pseudo-Spilman leaves, one member per leaf. JIT channels off. Target 512 members, tested at 128 or 256.
  • One funder per factory: the host, who signs funding only after the whole tree is signed. Members start at zero.
  • The factory key always needs everyone's signature. No threshold key.
  • Lightning's standard taproot channel format for the channel; the old format archived and recoverable.
  • Our own odd envelope (type 52791) is the message specification; bLIP-56 is an adapter; signatures are always over our native bytes (owner chose "option 3" after a comparison, 12 Nov; spec/messages.md). The 4-byte tag stays for Slice 1; the 32-byte protocol id is signed only together with renaming the tags, once the shared name is agreed with SuperScalar.
  • The standard is layered: transport, envelope, group coordination (a separate library), SuperScalar operations, channels, discovery, each with formats, state machines, acceptance rules and vectors (12 Nov; spec/PROTOCOL.md).
  • Long-term aim: factories as a core part of Lightning. Path: our odd type now, bLIP-56's even type via the adapter, our own feature bit, then a BOLT, as dual funding and splicing went (12 Nov; PLAN.md).
  • Lightning's own wire language; factory messages inside one custom message type.
  • Members adding their own coins, several hosts per factory, swap-in and swap-out: after the first release.

Safety

  • One member-side rule before every signature. Save before send. Signing secrets never stored. A restored backup never publishes its own state.
  • Exit fees are built in from the host's side, and tree transactions carry pay-to-anchor outputs so anyone can raise a fee. No on-chain reserve is required or recommended in 1.0.0; only a member who chooses to leave alone pays fees (owner decision 6, 11 Nov). In 1.1.0 a member holding more than its coverage keeps a small on-chain coin (decision 5).
  • About two weeks to respond to an old state in 1.0.0: the channel's revocation window, set by the terms; the design's figure, not measured (spec/safety-rule.md section 7). It holds below a timed layer only if the channel engine keeps every old anchor current; in 1.1.0 a member above its coverage may have under a day. The watch engine can also run as a separate process.
  • New joining or safety rules are normally changes to the safe-to-sign code itself, which is kept as one small, tested module.

Forming a factory

  • A lobby like a game server, with member conditions that are locked in. Gathering is long; signing is short at a scheduled start; the host sets both timers.
  • Newcomers with no funds are the main audience; each host sets their share of seats.
  • A lobby is listed or unlisted, and open or by invitation (QR invitations).
  • Names, chat and rule-change requests can each be switched off by the host. A member may attach a proven or free-text identity; identity is new per lobby by default.
  • Listings carry fixed terms, ranges, size and start time; live details come from the host. Hosts may publish a track record.
  • All four rule profiles ship: Safety, Standard, Starter, Circle. Deposits come in the release after the first.
  • No voting and no vote-to-remove. The host removes people; each member has a personal block list; history is kept locally.
  • Discovery over Lightning first, Nostr as an optional annex (12 Nov; spec/discovery.md draft 2): one odd feature bit, no host card; a host is whoever sends lobby beacons; beacons relayed among factory-capable nodes; opt-in store nodes serve listings; a pool request for catch-up and light clients. Proofs (anchors) are needed only on Nostr. The demo ships the bit, direct queries and Nostr; beacons and store nodes next. Live state, joining and signing stay direct.
  • The lobby and signing coordination are written so they can become their own library.

Hosts

  • A host links to a regular Lightning node through an adapter: LND first, then Core Lightning, then others. Later the built-in node holds ordinary channels itself.

  • Work proceeds in vertical slices on signet as soon as the engines they need are tested: form a factory, then a payment, then leaving alone. (11 Nov)

Working method

  • The owner handles outside review. This session's job is to bring the software to release candidates fit for review.
  • New rules and features go through SIPs (SuperScalar Improvement Proposals), modelled on BIPs and bLIPs. Approval and shipping are separate; the record of ACKs and NACKs is kept.
  • Review: several different AI models on every engine as it is built; outside human reviewers and red-teamers before real funds.
  • Hand-off notes are local documents the owner points the other session to.
  • Chain servers: well-known public ones first (checked list in CHAIN-SERVERS.md); our own around launch. No Bitcoin node of our own for now.
  • Reminders: in-app countdowns first; Nostr, email and phone notifications later.
  • Imported SuperScalar files are never edited here. Changes are requested from the SuperScalar repo.
  • Every safety rule has a test and a run with the rule removed that must fail.
  • Illustrated pages keep earlier versions behind a version switch.
  • Claims are checked against primary sources where money depends on them, and marked when they are not.
6. How sure we are

Checked today against a primary source or by running it

  • Lightning's message format, type ranges, typed fields and the odd/even rule (the messaging specification).
  • Peer-storage messages exist in the specification.
  • Core Lightning's dual-funding code received security fixes in four releases from August to November 2026 (its release notes).
  • LND has hold, settle and cancel for invoices built in (its interface definition).
  • Everything in section 4 marked as run, built or read on 2026-11-11.

From secondary sources only

  • Lightning's taproot channel format was merged into the specification in 2026 and ships in LND and Eclair.
  • LND is about 90% of public nodes; Core Lightning about 5%.
  • Core Lightning holds payments through a community plugin.
  • A WebSocket carrier for Lightning connections is not standardised.

Our own reasoning, not yet reviewed

  • The scheme for proving who broke a signing round.
  • That a member adding coins to their own leaf leaves other members unaffected.
  • That swap-in is safe under three conditions (confirmations first, on-chain timeout after the channel deadline, refund after timeout). The technique is long established; our statement of it was not re-checked against sources.
  • Estimates of size and effort.

Not yet known

  • Whether Eclair, LDK-based nodes and Nostr Wallet Connect can hold payments open in a way we can use.
  • The timelock and deadline numbers for 1.0.0's advanceless trees, and later for 1.1.0's timed levels.
  • How members who are offline at rotation are reached.
7. Risks we are carrying
  1. The chosen leaf design is proven only for building and one-process signing. One member per pseudo-Spilman leaf works at up to 1000 members in that test. Still untested at size: signing by separate parties, state changes, a member building its own copy, and anything on a chain. The SuperScalar session is running the whole-program test.
  2. The channel update routine must be exactly right through crashes and reconnects.
  3. Channel state cannot be rebuilt from a recovery phrase.
  4. Members must be reachable at rotation and must watch the chain.
  5. Deadlines, timelocks and factory lifetime must agree for every tree shape.
  6. Public chain servers can hide a transaction.
  7. The imported tree code still has open design issues upstream.
  8. Gathering many people to sign at once, with people who join and never sign.
  9. Privacy: a host sees all of its members' payments.
8. Practical facts
  • Builds and tests run on Gazpacho's own build server, in one folder. The server used earlier is left to the SuperScalar session.
  • The project runs no Bitcoin node of its own for now. It uses public chain servers, on signet (CHAIN-SERVERS.md).
  • Commits are authored as 8144225309 <8144225309@users.noreply.github.com>.
  • Automated checks run on GitHub for every push: a normal build and one with memory-error checkers.
  • The owner's machine is sometimes short of memory; long-running local helpers may be stopped. Pages open from disk without them.
  • Test transactions use a fee rate of 0.1 satoshi per virtual byte to preserve test coins (owner's instruction, 11 Nov). Anchor outputs for adding fees later are an accepted option. If a transaction at that rate does not spread, find out why before paying more.
  • Nothing is posted publicly, and nothing public is started, without the owner's say.
  • The build server allows each agent one SSH connection per 60 seconds. Long jobs (mutation runs, scale checks) are started detached and their results read later, not held open over a connection. (Found 12 Nov)

Plan

The mission, the decisions in force in one table, and the milestones.

Gazpacho wallet: plan · docs/PLAN.md

Updated 2026-11-12. This is the short plan. The reasoning and detail live in the documents listed at the end.

Mission

Get SuperScalar into people's hands. Gazpacho is a professional proof-of-concept wallet and the flagship way into SuperScalar: one program that lets anyone join a channel factory or host one, with no Bitcoin node, no separate Lightning node and no modified node software required.

What it is
  • Member and host in one program. "Host" is what the SuperScalar design calls the LSP; "member" is its client.
  • Self-contained. Its own small Lightning node for the factory channel, an ordinary on-chain wallet, and a chain view through public Electrum-style servers or the user's own node.
  • C throughout, with a local web interface as the screens.
  • Signet first. Mainnet code paths exist but are switched off in releases until the bar for real funds is met.
  • Quality first, no deadline.
Decisions in force
Area Decision
Source SuperScalar's low-level files imported unchanged as a pinned copy; everything above them written new
Structure A core library with no outside access (events in, actions out), ports for storage, chain and network, thin programs on top
Tree 1.0.0 trees are advanceless: every interior level fixed, no timed levels (owner decision 9, 2026-11-12). Timed levels and layer updates arrive with 1.1.0 and liquidity sales. Extending a factory always means rotation. Pseudo-Spilman leaves, one member per leaf. JIT channels off
Funding One funder per factory, the host, who signs funding only after the whole tree is signed. Members start at zero
Messages Lightning's own wire language; factory messages inside one custom odd message type (52791), which is the specification; bLIP-56 through an adapter
Channel Lightning's standard taproot channel format; the old format archived and recoverable
Safety One member-side rule before every signature; save before send; signing secrets never stored; a restored backup never publishes
Forming a factory A lobby: long gathering, short signing at a scheduled start; listed or unlisted, open or by invitation; member conditions locked in; no voting; host removal and personal block lists; four rule profiles
Changing the rules later A public proposal process modelled on BIPs and bLIPs
Host's link to Lightning An adapter to a regular node: LND first, then Core Lightning, then others. Later the built-in node does it alone
Away protection About two weeks to open the app; the watch engine can also run as a separate process
Reminders The core knows every date by which the user must act
Finding hosts Discovery over Lightning first: one feature bit, direct queries, signed beacons and opt-in store nodes; Nostr as an optional annex, where listings need a coin or channel anchor (spec/discovery.md). Live state, joining and signing stay direct. Slice 1 uses none of it: the member is given the host's address
Seats Free in 1.0.0, protected by invitation-only lobbies, host removal before the start, standby seats and the signing time budget. Next a proof of control of a coin or channel for public lobbies, with a share of newcomer seats that need none. Later the host chooses per lobby in its terms (owner decision 10)
Protocol id factory_protocol_id = SHA-256 of SuperScalar/1, shared with the SuperScalar projects (owner decision 8)
Not in the first release Swap-in and swap-out, a watching service, members adding their own coins, several hosts per factory
Old projects Soup wallet is mothballed and kept for reference. The SuperScalar repo is maintained by another session
Milestones
Outcome State
M0 Reference code run end to end; foundations shown to separate; reference values recorded Done
M0b Foundations imported into this repo with a build and a reference test Done
M1 Core skeleton and simulator (merged 11 Nov; a follow-up, pull request 9, hardens restart and adds faults the engines need); signing round engine; a fixed-tree factory created in the simulator with funding signed last In progress. The signing round engine is built on the build server but not yet in a pull request (SLICE1-WORK.md)
Slice 1 Form a factory on signet: lobby, signing, funding last, shown in the local web interface After M1
M2 Channel engine on the standard taproot format; payment engine; invariants on
Slice 2 A payment between two members through the host, on signet After M2
M3 Exit, watch and schedule engines; crash and cheating runs pass
Slice 3 Leaving alone: a member exits on signet with the host stopped After M3
M4 Rotation into the next factory (1.0.0 trees are advanceless: fixed levels only). Timed levels and layer updates are 1.1.0 work, with liquidity sales
M5 Hardening of storage, transport and chain view; Electrum backend; a node of our own for controlled-chain tests
M6 First release candidate on signet: lobby listings, host service, LND adapter, full local web interface, our public host

Slices are defined in SLICES.md. Each pulls a thin, real version of the outer pieces forward so there is something to run early; the simulator stays the main safety net.

After the release candidate

More node adapters and a browser of hosts; swap-in and swap-out; a watching service; the self-contained host.

Towards a Lightning standard (owner, 2026-11-12). The long-term aim is factories as a core part of Lightning. The path, as dual funding and splicing went: our own odd message type now (spec/messages.md); bLIP-56's even type through the adapter; a feature bit of our own; then a BOLT (spec/PROTOCOL.md).

Steps towards real funds
  1. Remaining design fixes, including the SuperScalar requests marked "before real funds".
  2. Legal advice before anyone hosts on mainnet. Whether running a host, or publishing host software, brings duties under money-transmission or similar law in the places it is used. Prosecutors in the Samourai case treated developers of non-custodial software as money transmitters. Gazpacho does not give legal advice; this step is advice from a lawyer, for the project and for hosts (OPEN-QUESTIONS.md).
  3. An outside audit.
  4. A capped beta.

Release In it
1.0.0 The milestones above. One member per leaf. Fees (proposed, owner to confirm): per payment, and an optional joining fee from the first payment received. Payout to one wallet. No on-chain reserve

How work proceeds
  1. Specification, written before code.
  2. Review of the specification by an agent that did not write it.
  3. Build, against ENGINEERING-CHECKLIST.md, including the builder's own mutation testing.
  4. Pull request, kept small; automated checks must pass.
  5. Review of the code by an agent that did not write it, which tries to break it.
  6. Fixes, then a second review of what changed.
  7. Merge, then the knowledge base and handbook are updated.

Work that does not depend on the server (specifications, research, reviews of text) runs alongside work that does (building and testing), so the critical path is never waiting for a document.

How work is checked
  • Every safety rule has a test, and a run with the rule removed that must fail.
  • A simulator runs whole factories in one process with dropped messages, crashes at every save point and scripted cheating, checking the invariants after every step.
  • The real programs run on regtest, then signet and testnet.
  • Each engine gets an independent review before it is built on.

Design

The design currently in force, consolidated.

Gazpacho design · docs/DESIGN.md

The current design, consolidated. 2026-11-11; owner decisions of 11 and 12 Nov added 2026-11-12; contradictions found by the gap review corrected 2026-11-12.

How it got here, with dates, findings and superseded ideas, is in RECORD.md. Where the two differ, this file is the one in force. The illustrated version is design.html.

1. What Gazpacho is

One program that lets a person join a SuperScalar channel factory as a member or run one as a host. It carries its own small Lightning node for factory channels and an ordinary on-chain wallet, and reads the chain through Electrum-style servers or the user's own node. It needs no modified node software. First releases are signet-only; the code for mainnet exists and is switched off.

2. Where the code comes from
  • Imported unchanged from SuperScalar, pinned to a known version under core/superscalar/: tree building, group signing, timelock counter, ladder, transaction and script building, adaptor signatures, key derivation, recovery phrases, address and invoice encoding, fee helpers. 38 files. A manifest records each file's checksum, and a script refreshes the copy.
  • Written new: everything above that. SuperScalar's host and member code is the reference for behaviour, not a source of code.
  • Not edited here: a change to an imported file is requested from the SuperScalar repo. If it cannot wait it goes in a short, listed patch queue.
  • Reference results recorded from real SuperScalar runs catch any drift when the import is updated.
3. Layers
Layer Contents Source
L5 Programs gazpacho command line, host service, local web interface new
L4 Ports storage, chain view, network, clock, randomness, host's Lightning node new
L3 Node sessions, journal, timers, on-chain wallet, host terms, member and host roles new
L2 Engines signing round, channel, payment, exit, watch, lifecycle, schedule new
L1 Factory model tree, timelocks, leaves, exits, ladder imported
L0 Primitives signatures, hashing, transactions, scripts, group signing imported

L0 to L3 are the core: portable C with no system calls. Everything is C except the screens of the local web interface.

The lobby, the signing round engine and the proof-of-fault scheme are written without dependence on the factory code so they can be split out as a coordination library.

4. The core contract

An event goes in and a list of actions comes out. The core never waits and never touches the outside world.

  • Events: a message arrived, a timer fired, something happened on chain, the user asked for something, storage confirmed a save.
  • Actions: save records as one atomic batch, send a message, broadcast a transaction, watch a script, set a timer, tell the user.
  • Save before send. The program around the core carries out actions in order and sends nothing until the save before it is safely stored. Enforced in one place for every engine.
  • Storage is a journal. Saves go to storage at once, in the order they were made, and become durable in that order. A send, broadcast or notification waits until every save made before it is durable. Confirmations arrive in order; one out of order is an error.
  • A save's identity is never reused. Each is numbered by the runner with the program's start count and a sequence. A late confirmation from before a restart is ignored and can never release anything.
  • A refused step is fatal for that node. If the runner cannot accept a step, the node's memory is ahead of what was saved, so the program stops the node and rebuilds it from storage. A storage failure does the same.
  • A node has a start step that returns actions, so after a restart it can re-arm timers, watch again and resend what was saved but never sent.
  • A timer that times out a message comes after that message in the list of actions, so a slow save cannot make a deadline pass before the request has left.
  • Randomness and time are handed to the core, so a whole factory can be simulated repeatably.
5. Engines
Engine Job Specification
Signing round Every group signature: propose, nonces, partial signatures, combine. One engine for creation, leaf changes, layer updates, rotation and close spec/signing-round.md
Channel One update routine: save, sign, verify the other side's cancellation of the old state, save, release our own. Updates arriving mid-update are queued To write
Payment Invoices; incoming and outgoing payments; deadlines derived from exit time To write
Exit Plans leaving alone: which transactions, in what order, when each is valid, how fees are paid To write
Watch Knows every output that matters and the response if it is spent. Can run as a separate process To write
Lifecycle Lobby, creation, liquidity changes, rotation, close docs/LOBBY.md for the lobby; rest to write
Schedule Computes every date by which the user must act To write
6. Safety

The full definition is spec/safety-rule.md. In brief:

  • One rule before every signature: sign only if, afterwards, the member can still leave alone, in time, with at least what they are owed, whatever everyone else does.
  • A member's software signs only transactions it rebuilt itself, recognises its outputs by its own keys, and never helps create an output without already holding its signed exit from it.
  • The factory key always needs everyone's signature. No threshold key.
  • Sign before fund: the host signs funding only after the whole tree is signed and verified.
  • Every partial signature is verified as it arrives.
  • Signing secrets are never stored. An interrupted round is abandoned.
  • A restored backup never publishes its own channel state automatically. It asks the host to prove the latest and waits for the host to close. If the host has not closed by its forcing date, publishing is offered as the person's own choice, with the risk stated (spec/exit.md 5.7).
  • The exit deadline is the leaf's timeout. Every tree output carries a host-only timeout; the binding one is the member's leaf's, at E − 10·d (E the end height, d the leaf's depth), and the member's commitment must be buried before it. The member starts leaving alone by that height less burial, one confirmation allowance per missing transaction (d + 3 of them) and margins: 50 to 82 blocks before E at Slice 1's signet constants (spec/exit.md 4, spec/safety-rule.md C5).
  • A locked wallet keeps watching (from Slice 3): a watcher publishes the member's tree path and answers old host states from already-signed transactions, but the last step, the member's own commitment, needs the passphrase by a stated unlock_by (spec/exit.md 5.6). Its files never hold a commitment of the member's own.
  • Exit fees are built in from the host's side, with pay-to-anchor outputs so anyone can raise a fee. No on-chain reserve is required or recommended in 1.0.0 (owner decision 6); in 1.1.0 a member above its coverage keeps a small on-chain coin (decision 5).
  • Away protection: about two weeks to respond to an old state in 1.0.0, the channel's revocation window (the design's figure, not measured; spec/safety-rule.md sections 7 and 8). In 1.1.0 a member above its coverage may have under a day.
  • A new safety or joining rule is normally a reviewed change to the safe-to-sign module, proposed through a SIP.
7. The factory
  • 1.0.0 trees are advanceless (owner decision 9, 2026-11-12): every interior level is fixed, with no timed (Decker-Wattenhofer) levels. Timed levels and layer updates arrive with 1.1.0, together with liquidity sales. Extending a factory's life always means rotation to a new funding output; refresh in place is never used. This replaces the earlier "full layered tree in the first release".
  • Pseudo-Spilman leaves, one member per leaf. Payments never change the tree. In 1.0.0 nothing changes the tree after creation; liquidity changes arrive with 1.1.0.
  • One funder per factory: the host. Members start at zero and are paid in over Lightning.
  • The tree's shape for each size is fixed by the host's terms; members are placed in an agreed order, host first.
  • Shape rules, from the scale check: a level is at most 15 wide; a wide level is used only where every subtree under it has more members than the width; two-way trees are not used at size (27 days to exit at 512 members); each timed level adds 3 days to an exit, so in 1.0.0 every level is fixed. Small factories (4, 8, 32) have their own shapes.
  • Payment deadlines are computed from the factory's exit time plus a margin; payments with less headroom are refused.
  • A member's software exits automatically before expiry if nothing else has happened.
  • At wind-down a member pays their balance into the next factory over Lightning, or co-signs a close that funds the next factory directly.
  • Target size 512 members; routine tests at smaller sizes.
  • Not in the first release: JIT channels, swap-in and swap-out, members adding their own coins, several hosts per factory.
  • Shared leaves come with sales in 1.1.0 (agreed with SuperScalar, 11 Nov). 1.0.0 has one member per leaf (K = 1). In 1.1.0 shared leaves (K > 1) arrive with sales of receiving room under the design's "limited coverage": each member's coverage is fixed when the factory is built and shown beside the balance. Each sale spends only the host's spare liquidity and makes a new channel for the buyer; existing channels never move, so a member can hold several channels in one factory (ux/decisions.md 5).
8. Messages and channel format
  • A Gazpacho connection is an ordinary Lightning peer connection that also carries factory messages.
  • Lightning's encrypted transport with the host's key pinned; over TCP, and over WebSocket where needed.
  • Lightning's message shape, session start and feature flags. No version numbers: unknown odd fields are skipped, unknown even ones refused.
  • Lightning's standard channel messages, and a taproot channel for the channel inside a leaf that departs from Lightning's standard taproot format in two places (spec/exit.md 10.1): its funding output is the leaf's output, tweaked with the host's timeout leaf (CH-3), not a BIP 86 key; and the member's commitment pays a fee fixed in advance and carries an anchor a member without coins can use, preferably as a TRUC transaction with a member-keyed anchor (CH-2). The old SuperScalar channel format is archived and can be reinstated behind the channel engine's format interface.
  • Factory messages travel inside one custom message type, with an envelope naming the protocol, the factory and the message kind.
  • Large signing data is split across messages with a completion marker.
  • A member's backup is held by the host using Lightning's peer-storage messages.
  • Encoders and decoders are to be generated from tables in spec/, with test vectors and fuzzing. No generator exists yet: tools/spec-vectors.py only prints vectors, and the transport's code is written by hand.
  • The message specification is spec/messages.md. Our own odd envelope (type 52791) is the specification; bLIP-56 is carried by an adapter, and signatures are always over our native bytes (owner's choice, 2026-11-12). It is offered to the SuperScalar repo as the shared format.
  • A layered standard (spec/PROTOCOL.md): transport, envelope, group coordination (a separate library), SuperScalar operations, channels and discovery are separate layers, each with its formats, state machines, acceptance rules and test vectors (owner, 2026-11-12).
9. Storage and backup
  • An append-only journal of typed records with periodic snapshots, encrypted at rest.
  • The encrypted journal is also the backup: one copy with the host, one the user can export.
  • A restored copy is never used to publish a channel state.
10. Chain view
  • Three choices: public Electrum-style servers (default), the user's own server, the user's own Bitcoin node.
  • Gazpacho checks every confirmation against block headers and a Merkle proof, and counts confirmations along linked headers. On testnet and mainnet that makes an invented confirmation cost proof of work at the difficulty it claims (whether that difficulty is the one the network required is not checked). On signet, where blocks are signed and the signature is not checked, servers that collude can still invent one; what protects the user there is comparing servers of different operators. Detail in notes/chain-port.md section 6.
  • A server can hide a transaction, so Gazpacho asks more than one. Hosts should use their own node.
  • Default list: well-known public servers; one of ours is added around launch.
11. Forming a factory

Full detail in docs/LOBBY.md.

  • A lobby, like a game server. Start rule: a target, a minimum and a deadline. Reaching the target starts at once; at the deadline it starts if the minimum is met, and otherwise quits or carries members over.
  • Gathering is long; signing is short and automatic within each member's conditions.
  • A member's conditions are locked in, and are checked against the real tree at signing.
  • Listed or unlisted; open or by invitation (QR codes). A listing is one signed record, the same bytes on every path (spec/discovery.md draft 2): asked of the host over Lightning; announced by a small signed beacon relayed among factory-capable nodes and served by opt-in store nodes; and, optionally, on Nostr. One odd feature bit marks a factory-capable node; a host is whoever sends beacons. The signet demo wallet ships the bit, direct queries and Nostr; beacons and store nodes come next. Slice 1 uses none of these: the member is given the host's address directly (SLICES.md). Which release the demo wallet is remains open (OPEN-QUESTIONS.md).
  • No voting. The host removes people; each member has a personal block list; history is kept locally.
  • Host switches for names, chat and rule-change requests. Optional self-identification. Identity is new per lobby by default.
  • Four rule profiles: Safety, Standard, Starter, Circle.
  • Listings carry fixed terms, ranges, size and start time. Live details come from the host.
  • Terms (owner decision 1, spec/terms.md): a lobby's outer rules are fixed when it opens; adjustable terms move only inside signed ranges; values freeze at the ready check; the outer rules' digest is the rules fingerprint and the lobby's identity. Changing an outer rule makes a new lobby.
12. The host
  • Runs Gazpacho continuously, with its own Bitcoin node recommended.
  • Links its factory to the rest of Lightning through a regular node, by an adapter with four operations: pay an invoice; create an invoice whose secret the node does not know and hold the payment; settle it; cancel it. LND first, then Core Lightning, then others. See docs/HOST-NODES.md.
  • Later the built-in node holds ordinary channels itself and no separate node is needed.
  • Publishes signed, versioned terms. May publish its funded factories as a track record.
  • Fees in 1.0.0 (proposed after the owner's direction, decision 2; owner to confirm): a per-payment fee (a base amount plus a proportion), and an optional joining fee taken from the first payment received. The fee for extra receiving room comes in 1.1.0. A host cannot take any charge the member has not signed.
13. The user's side
  • No role question (owner decision 4): everyone can be member and host. The app never asks "member or host?"; hosting is always visible, with a calm starting page until the person hosts something.
  • Where coins go (owner decision 6): 1.0.0 pays out to one wallet; 1.1.0 adds derived addresses of the same wallet per factory; an outside or cold wallet comes later. A destination is fixed before the factory forms, because pre-signed transactions fix it, and an outside address is proven before use. The tree builder must accept a destination per member (a request to SuperScalar, handoff/2026-11-12-to-superscalar.md, E2).
  • Screens: a local web interface served by the C program on the local machine only, with sign-in by the wallet passphrase, which also decrypts the files (owner decisions 13 and 14; first run protected by a one-use start token the program opens itself), a session token kept out of cookies, and refusal of requests from other sites (spec/local-api.md). Slice 1 ships with it, and every Slice 1 action is done through it (owner decision 11). Keys and channel state never enter the browser, with one exception: the 24 words may be shown in it, and typed into it for the backup check and a restore, under fixed key-handling rules (owner decision 12, 2026-11-12, which replaces the earlier "never"): loopback only; shown at creation and on reveal after a fresh sign-in; never in URLs, logs, events, caches or page storage; no clipboard copy by default; cleared from the page after display; the check never says which position is wrong; attempt limits; the program wipes its copies after use (spec/local-api.md section 12.7).
  • Reminders: countdowns in the app first. Other channels and ways to act while away are in docs/AWAY.md.
14. Changing the rules

SuperScalar Improvement Proposals, docs/sips/. Request in a set format, open discussion, ACK or NACK on record, then separate decisions on approval and on shipping.

15. Testing

docs/TESTING.md, in two parts. What runs today: reference values, wallet key vectors, the core and simulator tests with four invariants, sanitizer builds on GitHub. Planned: seven levels, a simulator that injects network faults, crashes and cheating, nine invariants checked after every step, and two tests for every safety rule.

17. Not designed yet

Safe-to-sign rule

Specification: the checks run before every signature.

The safe-to-sign rule · spec/safety-rule.md

Specification draft 4, 2026-11-12. Status: draft 3 brought in line with the four companion specifications after a third, joint review (docs/reviews/spec-review-3.md, findings F1 to F28), which judged draft 3 "ready after listed changes" and found that no finding needs a different cryptographic design. Draft 4 has not itself been reviewed. Nothing here has been reviewed outside this project. Values marked proposed, owner to confirm are Slice 1 choices the owner has not objected to and has not yet confirmed.

This document defines the check a member's software runs before it contributes to any signature, the matching rules for the host, and what the rule cannot protect against. The code that implements it is one module, the only place where "is this safe to sign?" is decided.

Companion documents: signing-round.md (draft 4), messages.md (draft 2), terms.md (draft 2) and journal.md (draft 3), all brought in line with this draft; exit.md (draft 2), storage-encryption.md (draft 3) and local-api.md (draft 4), which the alignment section below uses; THREAT-MODEL.md (docs/).

Changes after cross-spec review 1

Alignment fixes from docs/reviews/review-cross-spec-1.md, made in one pass with exit.md, storage-encryption.md, local-api.md and journal.md. No new draft number: no check changes.

Alignment with `exit.md` draft 2 (2026-11-12)

Draft 4 is brought in line with spec/exit.md draft 2 and its review (docs/reviews/review-exit-d1.md, section 4 items 1 to 3, finding F15). The checks keep their shape, but C8's end-height test becomes stricter: it is judged against the leaf's timeout, not the end height, with exit.md's margins. The timeouts, the exit time and its margins now say exactly what the import builds and what the exit engine computes; a Slice 1 build that coded draft 4's numbers must change them.

Point Change Where
Timeout heights A fixed kickoff at depth j spends an output timing out at E − 5·(2j − 1); the leaf transaction and the channel output at H_leaf = E − 10·d, the binding one. Draft 4's "5 blocks per tree transaction" fitted kickoff-and-state pairs only C5, section 12 value 2
Exit time and margins 1.0.0's exit time is n·A plus burial (B − 1), with n = d + 3 and no relative delays, judged against H_leaf; the margin is exit.md's M (M_safe + M_lag + M_spike, 14 on signet), not 6 Section 7, C8, section 12 value 6
Host rules H14 to H18 of exit.md section 9 listed; H6 noted as payout-only Section 10
Exclusions Locked past unlock_by; no bundle; fees above the anchors and the pinning of keyless anchors; second-stage payments after a breach while locked; an eclipse Section 11
